[MALE] And this isn't a Jersey plate. Or New York. Not with just two letters. It's three and three, not two and three.

[NARRATOR] Teddy’s voice rings out over the line, defensive but not unkind.

[MALE] It's what he said.

[NARRATOR] The room falls silent. The weight of the moment presses down on them all, each man calculating the cost of the truth.

[MALE] Then what the fuck is it?

[NARRATOR] Jimmy’s patience wears thin, his voice cracking slightly.

[MALE] The fuck should I know?

[NARRATOR] Abruzzo’s face hardens, his jaw clenched tight.

[MALE] You're fuckin lying.

[NARRATOR] And just like that, the fragile thread of trust snaps, leaving nothing but questions in its wake. The night stretches on, indifferent to their troubles, as the city sleeps and the stars blink coldly overhead.

[NARRATOR] The quiet hum of the evening settled over the airfield like a soft blanket, and the military transport, its tail bearing the distinctive Air Force mark, eased down through the scattered clouds with the gentle grace of a gull returning home. The sky, painted in the amber glow of late afternoon, stretched endlessly above, and the aircraft carved a slow, steady path toward the runway, its engines whispering promises of arrival.

[NARRATOR] On the tarmac below, a gathered crowd of law enforcement stood in quiet formation, their silhouettes sharp against the fading light. Richie's entire staff of detectives, along with Toback, the district attorney, and several customs agents, watched with a mixture of anticipation and concern as the plane taxied toward them. The weight of the moment pressed gently on their shoulders, each officer feeling the significance of what was about to unfold.

[NARRATOR] Far away, in the quiet suburbs of Teaneck, New Jersey, the sounds of everyday life continued undisturbed. Birds chirped lazily from the trees, and the distant hum of a lawn mower blended with the soft rustle of leaves. Inside the modest home of Mama Lucas, the kitchen filled with the comforting aroma of coffee and the warmth of familiar company.

[NARRATOR] Frank's mother sat across from Ana, her eyes bright with memories as she showed her some old photographs of Frank as a boy. The images, faded with time, captured moments of laughter and innocence, and the women smiled softly as they sipped their coffee. The gentle clink of porcelain cups was the only sound breaking the peaceful atmosphere until a light tap on the glass French door drew their attention.

[NARRATOR] Trupo stood just outside, his hand raised in a casual wave, accompanied by other police officers moving quietly past. The scene was one of quiet normalcy, yet beneath the surface, something stirred—an unspoken tension that would soon ripple through the stillness.

[NARRATOR] Back at the airport, an Army captain approached Richie's group, his steps deliberate and confident. The director of the Essex County Narcotics Bureau stepped forward, his voice carrying authority as he introduced himself. The exchange was brief but meaningful, a formal acknowledgment that set the stage for what was to come.

[NARRATOR] Inside Mama Lucas's home, the front door swung open with a gentle push, and Trupo made his presence known by waving a search warrant at Julie. The New York cops followed closely behind, their movements precise as they fanned out to secure the area. Trupo and his detectives headed upstairs, their footsteps echoing softly on the wooden stairs as they began their search.

[NARRATOR] In the hangar later, the plane had been brought inside and was being meticulously stripped apart, much like a car abandoned by thieves. The cabin, once filled with the chatter of passengers, now lay open and vulnerable. Seats were carefully removed and inspected, carpeting was pulled up with delicate precision, and panels were unscrewed to reveal hidden compartments. The lavatories were dismantled, each component examined with the care of someone searching for secrets buried deep.

[NARRATOR] Downstairs at the house, Ana and Frank's mother sat guarded and silent, their ears straining to catch the sounds of activity from above. The muffled commotion of Trupo and his detectives ransacking a bedroom filled the air, a stark contrast to the peaceful morning that had preceded it. The weight of the intrusion hung heavy in the atmosphere, as the ordinary rhythms of life were disrupted by the relentless pursuit of truth.

[NARRATOR] The morning light filtered through the drawn curtains, casting long shadows across the room as Trupo moved with purposeful urgency. His hands pulled open drawer after drawer, the wooden frames scraping against their tracks with a hollow echo. Clothes cascaded onto the floor in disarray, fabrics rustling softly before settling into crumpled heaps. He lifted an invitation to a United Nations ball, its elegant script catching the dim light for a moment before he tossed it down again with dismissive flair.

[NARRATOR] His fingers continued their search, more methodical now, until they brushed against something small and metallic tucked away in the back of a sock drawer. A safety deposit box key, its surface cool and slightly tarnished, caught his attention. He slipped it into his pocket with quiet satisfaction, the weight of it a secret promise.

[NARRATOR] The airport hangar stretched endlessly around him, its vast ceiling disappearing into shadows. The aircraft that had once been whole now lay broken and exposed, its engines and landing gear scattered like mechanical entrails across the concrete floor. Tires had been slit open and their contents sifted through by gloved hands, while a long nozzle plunged deep into the plane's toilet, pumping out its contents into waiting barrels.

[NARRATOR] Detectives moved through the wreckage with practiced efficiency, their flashlights cutting through the gloom as they fished through debris with careful precision. The metallic scent of oil and fuel hung heavy in the air, mingling with the sharp tang of cleaning solvents.

[NARRATOR] Later, in the modest home of Mama Lucas in Teaneck, New Jersey, Ana was led upstairs with trembling steps. The house had been transformed into a crime scene, furniture overturned and personal belongings scattered across every surface. She found herself standing before a circle of SIU detectives, their expressions grim as they waited for the NYPD officers to clear from the room.

[MALE] Your husband's illustrious career is over.

[FEMALE] What do you want from me?

[MALE] The Feds are going to come in and take it all. Everything.

[NARRATOR] Trupo's voice carried a venomous satisfaction as he circled around her slowly, each step deliberate and menacing.

[MALE] But not before I get my gratuity.

[FEMALE] Where's the money?

[FEMALE] There was some on that dresser, but it's gone now so I guess you—

[MALE] The money!

[NARRATOR] His shout cracked through the silence like a whip, making her flinch. He stepped closer, his presence overwhelming in the cramped space.

[MALE] The getaway money Frank and every other gangster keeps in his house!

[FEMALE] If you leave now, there's a chance Frank might not kill you—

[NARRATOR] Before she could finish, his hand shot out and slapped her hard across the face. The sound of flesh meeting palm echoed sharply, and she swayed slightly, her cheek already beginning to swell.

[NARRATOR] Downstairs, Mama Lucas knelt beside Ana, gently pressing a cool washcloth against her daughter's injured cheek. The sounds of destruction continued upstairs—the crash of furniture, the splintering of wood, the relentless pounding of sledgehammers against walls. Glass shattered somewhere in the distance, adding its crystalline crash to the symphony of ruin.

[FEMALE] I'm sorry.

[NARRATOR] Her whispered words were barely audible above the chaos, but Mama Lucas understood. The apology wasn't for the search itself, but for the devastation that had befallen their family.

[NARRATOR] In the hangar, Richie stood apart from the others, his solitary vigil growing more desperate with each passing moment. Around him, the mechanics and investigators continued their thorough dismantling of the aircraft. Every panel was being removed, every cavity probed and examined. What had once been a majestic plane was rapidly becoming nothing more than a skeleton of aluminum and wire.

[NARRATOR] His heart pounded in his chest as he watched them work, each revelation more troubling than the last. They had looked everywhere and found nothing, yet the weight of failure pressed down on him like a physical force.

[NARRATOR] Then his gaze settled on the military caskets being carefully loaded onto a transport truck. Armed soldiers stood guard around their solemn cargo, their faces impassive in the harsh fluorescent lighting. Something about the scene—the reverence, the finality—stopped Richie in his tracks.

[NARRATOR] The contrast between the sacred duty of carrying the fallen and the desperate scramble to uncover secrets hidden in the living weighed heavily on his conscience. In that moment, he understood that some things were meant to remain buried, and that the price of discovery might be higher than anyone had anticipated.

[NARRATOR] The morning light filters through the remnants of what was once a bedroom, now scattered and broken across the floor. The sound of a single dog barking carries softly through the air, a lonely note that drifts from the kennel out back. Trupo stands at the window, his silhouette framed against the pale glow, watching the shepherd press itself against the wooden slats, growling low in its throat.

[NARRATOR] The dog’s barks echo faintly, each one a quiet punctuation in the stillness of the house. Trupo’s hand rests lightly on the windowsill, his fingers tracing the rough wood as he studies the animal through the glass. The shepherd snarls, its teeth bared, but there is something almost pleading in the way it strains toward the fence, as if trying to communicate something urgent.

[NARRATOR] He steps away from the window and crosses the lawn with measured steps, each footfall soft against the dew-kissed grass. The morning air is cool and carries the scent of earth and leaves. As he approaches the kennel, the barking intensifies, becoming sharper, more insistent. The dog paces behind the fence, its movements restless and agitated.

[NARRATOR] Trupo circles around to the back of the kennel, his boots crunching lightly on the gravel path. He places his palm against the wooden frame and gives it a gentle push. The door creaks open just slightly, as though it has been waiting for this moment, held in place by nothing more than habit and rust.

[NARRATOR] Inside the hangar, the vast space hums with a different kind of tension. Richie rises slowly from where he had been standing, his movements deliberate and unhurried. The fluorescent lights overhead flicker softly, casting long shadows across the rows of coffins that line the concrete floor like silent sentinels.

[NARRATOR] He walks toward the nearest coffin with quiet purpose, each step echoing slightly in the cavernous room. The Army captain stands nearby, his expression stern and unyielding, arms crossed across his chest. Between them hangs an unspoken challenge, heavy with the weight of authority and defiance.

[NARRATOR] RICHIE speaks softly but clearly, his voice carrying across the space.

[MALE] Open it.

[NARRATOR] The captain regards him for a long moment, his gaze measuring and calculating. Then he shakes his head slightly.

[MALE] The warrant permits me to search the plane and its cargo.

[NARRATOR] But the captain doesn’t move to comply. Instead, he stands firm, his posture rigid with discipline. Richie’s eyes flick to the soldiers positioned around them, their rifles already shouldered, barrels glinting in the harsh light.

[NARRATOR] Without a word, Richie takes another step forward. Instantly, every rifle snaps up, trained on him with deadly precision. The safeties click off, and fingers tighten around triggers. The soldiers stand motionless, waiting only for the command to fire.

[NARRATOR] RICHIE holds their gaze, his own expression unreadable. For a heartbeat, the world seems suspended in that moment, caught between obedience and defiance.

[MALE] I don't need it.

[NARRATOR] Back outside, Trupo returns to the front of the kennel, his gun drawn. The shepherd snarls from behind the fence, its aggression now tempered by fear. Trupo raises his weapon and fires once, the shot sharp and final in the quiet morning air.

[NARRATOR] The dog collapses instantly, its body sliding against the fencing with a soft metallic clatter. Trupo lowers his gun and approaches the kennel once more. He pushes at the frame again, this time with more force, and it swings open fully.

[NARRATOR] Inside, beneath the overturned kennel, lies Frank’s hidden stash—packages and bundles carefully concealed beneath layers of dirt and debris. The contents are scattered now, revealed by violence and intrusion, their secrets laid bare beneath the cold morning light.

[NARRATOR] In the hangar, Richie kneels beside the coffin, his hands steady as he pulls the latches free. The metal groans softly under his touch, and he lifts the lid with quiet reverence. Inside, resting upon the velvet lining, is a long black body bag, sealed and solemn.

[NARRATOR] He parts the zipper carefully, drawing back the dark fabric to reveal what lies beneath. The remains of a young soldier are visible, pale and still, their uniform bearing the marks of service and sacrifice.

[NARRATOR] A voice cuts through the silence, calm but firm.

[MALE] That's enough.

[NARRATOR] The U.S. Attorney steps forward, his presence commanding immediate attention. The tension in the room shifts subtly, as if recognizing the end of one chapter and the beginning of another.

[NARRATOR] The fluorescent lights hummed overhead, casting a pale, unforgiving glow across the rows of metal desks and folding chairs arranged in the stark conference room of the airport hangar office. Outside, the low rumble of aircraft engines drifted across the tarmac, a distant reminder of the world beyond these walls where decisions carried weight and consequences rippled outward like stones dropped into still water. The room itself felt heavy with tension, its bare walls and industrial fixtures doing little to soften the gravity of the moment.

[MALE] There are drugs on that plane.

[NARRATOR] The words hung in the air like smoke, sharp and unyielding, and for a heartbeat, the only sound was the soft click of the US Attorney setting down his phone. His expression darkened, not with anger alone, but with something deeper—disbelief, perhaps, or the dawning realization that everything he thought he knew had just shifted beneath his feet.

[MALE] Shut the fuck up.

[NARRATOR] Richie obeyed, though barely. His jaw tightened, and he glanced sideways at Toback, who shifted uncomfortably in his seat, fingers drumming against his knee. The contempt in Richie’s eyes wasn’t lost on either of them, but it was directed not at the federal agents across the table—it was aimed at the system itself, at men who had never walked the streets they claimed to understand, yet now held the power to dismantle lives with a single signature.

[MALE] That’s how these events are being interpreted by General Easton in that call to me. That someone employed by this office believes the United States Army is in the drug trafficking business—and is trying to prove it by desecrating the remains of young men who’ve given their lives in the defense of democracy.

[NARRATOR] The room seemed to contract, the air growing thinner with each word. The US Attorney leaned forward, his voice dropping to a near whisper, as if speaking too loudly might shatter something fragile within the space between them.

[MALE] Is it any wonder then, because of your actions, the entire federal narcotics program is now in jeopardy of being dismantled as completely and enthusiastically as that fucking transport plane? That’s what you’ve accomplished, Mr. Roberts. Single-handedly.

[NARRATOR] Richie’s fingers curled around the edge of the table, knuckles whitening. He had heard threats before, issued them too, but there was something different in the tone now—something final. The weight of the accusation pressed down on him like a hand around his throat, and yet beneath it all, there was a strange calm, as if he had long since accepted that this moment was inevitable.

[MALE] I had good information the target of my investigation was bringing dope in on that plane.

[NARRATOR] The silence that followed was longer this time, deeper. None of the federal attorneys had ever heard the name before, and it showed in the way they exchanged glances—brief, uncertain, searching for confirmation that they weren’t missing something obvious.

[MALE] And that target is?

[NARRATOR] Richie exhaled slowly, as though the act of speaking the name might somehow make it real, or worse, make it disappear.

[MALE] Frank Lucas.

[NARRATOR] No one moved. No one breathed. The name echoed off the concrete walls like a challenge thrown into the void, and for the first time since the meeting had begun, the federal agents looked genuinely unsettled.

[MALE] Who?

[NARRATOR] The question came sharp and clipped, but even as it left the US Attorney’s lips, he could feel the ground shifting beneath him.

[MALE] Who’s Frank Lucas?

[NARRATOR] The room was silent save for the distant hum of machinery and the occasional creak of the old hangar doors swaying in the breeze. No one offered an answer. No one even seemed to know where to begin.

[MALE] Who’s he work for? Which family?

[NARRATOR] Richie’s lips curled into something that might have been a smile, bitter and knowing.

[MALE] He’s not Italian. He’s black.

[NARRATOR] The words settled over the room like a shroud. The kind of silence that followed wasn’t just absence of sound—it was the kind that carried meaning, that spoke of assumptions unspoken and prejudices unchallenged. The federal agents shifted in their seats, and for the first time, the balance of power in the room felt less certain.

[MALE] Is that supposed to be some kind of joke?

[NARRATOR] The US Attorney’s voice cracked slightly, betraying the frustration that had been building beneath his composed facade.

[MALE] You’re this close to the end of your career in law enforcement, you’re making jokes?

[NARRATOR] Richie opened his mouth, perhaps to respond, perhaps to laugh, but then closed it again. The weight of the moment pressed down on him, and he found himself thinking not of the case, not of the drugs or the plane or the military, but of something far simpler—the long road that had led him here, the choices that had brought him to this table, and the man whose name had just been spoken for the first time in this room.

[MALE] I—

[NARRATOR] His voice trailed off, swallowed by the oppressive quiet. Somewhere in the distance, a plane took flight, its engines fading into the sky, carrying with it secrets and lies and the fragile threads of justice that bound them all together. And in that moment, none of them—neither Richie nor Toback nor the federal agents—could pretend that anything would ever be the same.

[NARRATOR] The fluorescent lights hummed overhead, casting a pale glow across the conference room where shadows stretched long against the walls. The air carried that particular stillness that settles in just before something breaks, like the moment between a breath held and released. Richie had been speaking for some time now, his voice steady despite the weight of his words, describing a theory that seemed to bend the very fabric of what they thought they knew about the city's underworld.

[MALE] I believe Frank Lucas is above the mafia in the dope business. I believe he buys direct from the source in Southeast Asia, cuts out all the middlemen, and uses US military planes and personnel to bring pure No. 4 heroin into United States.

[NARRATOR] The words hung in the air like smoke, and around the table, faces shifted subtly, trying to make sense of something that felt almost too vast to comprehend. The U.S. Attorney leaned back in his chair, his expression unreadable, while Toback exchanged glances with the others—detectives whose jobs had taught them to be skeptical of anything that seemed too clean, too perfect.

[MALE] Richie has a lot of experience.

[NARRATOR] Toback's voice came softly, almost pleading, as if trying to shield his partner from whatever storm was gathering. The U.S. Attorney's eyes narrowed slightly, and for a moment, the only sound was the distant whisper of traffic filtering through the building's windows.

[MALE] Does he.

[NARRATOR] The question was quiet, but it carried the weight of dismissal. The room seemed to contract, the walls closing in just a fraction, as if the space itself were listening.

[MALE] And how many arrests has he made in his so-called investigation?

[NARRATOR] The challenge landed like a stone in still water, ripples spreading outward through the circle of men seated around the table. Richie's jaw tightened, but he didn't flinch. He had come here prepared, with facts tucked beneath his sleeves like armor.

[MALE] I was promised when I took this job, it was about real arrests.

[NARRATOR] His voice carried no anger—just exhaustion, the kind that comes from chasing ghosts too long. The U.S. Attorney studied him for a long moment, then turned his gaze to the others, as if seeking validation for the words that were about to follow.

[MALE] Does that mean none?

[NARRATOR] The silence that followed was heavy, broken only by the soft rustle of papers and the occasional creak of a chair. Richie straightened, his shoulders squaring, and for the first time, something dangerous flickered in his eyes—not rage, but resolve.

[MALE] I have cases against most of Frank's organization. Not him.

[NARRATOR] The statement was delivered with quiet precision, each word measured. The U.S. Attorney's lips curled into something that might have been a sneer, though it was difficult to tell in the dim light.

[MALE] Frank's organization.

[NARRATOR] He repeated it as though tasting the phrase, letting it roll off his tongue like a curse. The implication was clear—no one outside the black community had ever managed to dismantle what the Italian mob had built over decades. But here was Richie, suggesting that a single man had done what entire crime families could not.

[MALE] No fucking nigger has accomplished what the American Mafia hasn't in a hundred years!

[NARRATOR] The room erupted in tension, sharp and electric. Richie's hand twitched at his side, and the U.S. Attorney’s smile faded into something colder.

[MALE] Yeah, you'd know, sitting here, having never been on the street.

[NARRATOR] The accusation hung between them like a challenge thrown down in the dirt. The U.S. Attorney stood slowly, his chair scraping against the floor. His voice dropped to a whisper that somehow carried across the entire room.

[MALE] Lou, get this fucking kike out of here.

[NARRATOR] Richie moved before anyone could react. His fists flew in a blur of motion—quick, controlled, devastating. The sound of hands connecting with flesh echoed off the walls, and suddenly, Toback was shouting, pulling people apart, trying to separate two men who had crossed a line neither of them would ever uncross.

[MALE] He was out of line, Richie.

[NARRATOR] Toback said it as they walked briskly across the lobby, his tone urgent but measured. The polished floor reflected the harsh lights above, and the echo of their footsteps seemed unnaturally loud in the emptiness. Richie wasn’t really listening. His mind was elsewhere—on the cases he’d built, the evidence he’d gathered, and the bitter realization that none of it mattered when faced with prejudice dressed up as pragmatism.

[MALE] It's over. You're shut down.

[NARRATOR] Toback watched as Richie continued striding toward the exit, his coat flapping behind him like the wings of a bird unwilling to be caged. One by one, the detectives fell into step behind them, their faces grim with understanding. This wasn’t just about Frank Lucas anymore—it was about justice itself, and how easily it could be dismissed when it came wrapped in the wrong skin.

[NARRATOR] Outside, the night air was thick with humidity, carrying the distant rumble of engines and the occasional shout from the street below. In the terminal’s parking garage, Richie paused beside his car, looking up at the stars hidden behind layers of smog and citylight. The weight of defeat pressed down on him, but beneath it simmered something sharper—determination.

[NARRATOR] Meanwhile, far above the city, in a penthouse where luxury met isolation, Frank Lucas stood by the window, watching the world below with eyes that missed nothing. An airline representative had just left, and Doc—his longtime associate—waited quietly near the door, his posture rigid with concern.

[MALE] Frank?

[NARRATOR] The name barely escaped Doc’s lips, tinged with worry. Frank turned, and in that instant, his expression shifted—something unreadable passed through his features, like clouds racing across the moon.

[MALE] Ten million dollars means nothing to me.

[NARRATOR] The words were spoken softly, almost to himself, as he reached for the small device on the table. With practiced efficiency, he slapped a cartridge into the butt of a pistol, the click echoing faintly in the room. Ana sat at the edge of the couch, her eyes fixed on the floor, her hands trembling slightly. She had always known this moment would come, but knowing and living it were two different things entirely.

[MALE] Ana.

[NARRATOR] Frank’s voice softened just a fraction as he looked toward her, though his hands remained steady. She lifted her gaze, meeting his eyes for the briefest moment before dropping them again. There was fear there, yes—but also something deeper. Regret. Love. The knowledge that they had been given a chance to walk away, and instead, they had chosen to stay.

[MALE] It all just seems to get worse and worse.

[NARRATOR] Ana whispered it quietly, more to herself than to anyone else. And Frank, standing there with the weight of empire pressing down on his shoulders, knew she was right. Every victory came with a price. Every triumph carved another scar. Yet still, he pressed forward, driven by forces that even he couldn’t fully name.

[NARRATOR] The city sprawled endlessly beneath them, its lights blinking like stars fallen to earth. Somewhere in its depths, a battle was being waged—not with guns or money, but with truth and lies, loyalty and betrayal. And as Frank slipped the pistol into his waistband, he understood that the war was far from over. It had only just begun.

[NARRATOR] The dim glow of the hallway light cast long shadows across the polished banister as Frank descended the grand staircase, each step carrying him closer to the weight that waited below. The silence between the walls felt thick, almost reverent, as if the apartment itself held its breath. In the distance, the muffled hum of the city seeped through the double-pane windows, a distant reminder of the world beyond these gilded walls.

[MALE] Frank paused at the foot of the stairs, glancing back toward the living room where his mother sat alone in her chair, her silhouette framed by the soft amber light of the lamp beside her.

[NARRATOR] Mrs. Lucas remained seated, her hands folded neatly in her lap, her gaze fixed upon her son with a stillness that had not graced her features in years. The years had carved gentle lines around her eyes, but tonight they shone with something sharper—something that cut through the carefully constructed facade Frank had worn like armor.

[FEMALE] Mrs. Lucas studied him in a way she hadn't since he was little, when he would come home with scraped knees and muddy shoes, seeking her forgiveness and a warm meal. Now, her voice carried neither anger nor accusation, but a quiet certainty that settled into the space between them like dust motes in moonlight.

[FEMALE] If you'd have been a preacher, your brothers would be preachers. If you'd been a soldier, they'd be soldiers. Do you know that?

[NARRATOR] The words hung in the air, soft yet unyielding. Frank shifted slightly, unsure whether to sit or stand, caught between the boy who once looked up to her and the man who now stood before her—a man whose choices had ripened into consequences none of them could pluck away.

[FEMALE] They all came here because of you. You called and they came running. They look up to you. They expect you to always know what's best.

[NARRATOR] A pause followed, heavy with unspoken truths. The ticking of an old grandfather clock echoed faintly from somewhere in the apartment, marking time as if reluctant to move forward.

[FEMALE] But even they know you don't shoot cops. Even I know that. Ana knows it. You seem to be the only one who doesn't.

[NARRATOR] Her words struck deeper than any reprimand. Frank felt the ground beneath his feet grow uncertain, as though the foundation of his world had begun to shift. He opened his mouth to speak, but no sound escaped—only silence remained, vast and unbroken.

[MALE] Is that where I'm going?

[FEMALE] I never asked you where all this came from because I didn't want to hear you lie to me. Don't lie to me. Don't do that, too.

[NARRATOR] She rose then, slow and deliberate, her movements graceful despite the years that had bent her spine. Her voice did not waver, nor did it plead—it simply told, as one might recite a prayer or deliver a verdict.

[FEMALE] Do you really want to make things so bad for your family they'll leave you? Because they will. She will—pointing upward, toward the bedroom where Ana waited—knows it. I know I will.

[NARRATOR] Frank found himself struggling to meet her eyes. The truth of her words pressed against his chest like a hand around his heart. He nodded once, silently acknowledging the storm he had unleashed, and turned toward the front door. But as his fingers brushed the cold metal of the doorknob, something held him back—not fear, not guilt, but the fragile thread of love that still bound him to this place, to these people.

[NARRATOR] He hesitated there, motionless, letting the weight of his decision settle over him like a shroud. Then, slowly, he withdrew his hand and walked upstairs, leaving the door unopened. Trupo would live, at least for now, because of her.

[NARRATOR] Far below, in the sterile corridors of the Army Hospital, the night deepened its hold. Beneath the surface, in the basement where shadows pooled like ink, the body bags were lifted from their wooden coffins with mechanical precision. Set down on steel tables, the bags were unzipped, revealing the pale forms within. The morticians moved quietly, their hands steady as they began the solemn work of dressing the dead in fresh uniforms, applying makeup to faces gone slack and gray.

[NARRATOR] New white military caskets rolled in on wheeled platforms, their polished surfaces reflecting the harsh fluorescent lights overhead. Gold handles gleamed briefly before being tarnished by the touch of gloved hands. Each casket bore the weight of a life cut short, each one a vessel carrying home what remained of boys who had once laughed, loved, and dreamed beneath the same sun that now cast its indifferent gaze upon this place of farewells.

[NARRATOR] And somewhere above it all, in a penthouse lit by the flicker of dying embers, Frank stood at the window, watching the city breathe around him. The wind whispered through the glass, carrying with it the scent of rain and the promise of dawn. In the distance, a siren wailed—a sound that seemed to echo the ache in his chest. He closed his eyes and let the moment stretch before him, knowing that choices made in darkness often birthed consequences that lingered long after the light returned.

[NARRATOR] The night air around the army hospital carries a stillness, thick with reverence and the weight of unspoken ceremony. Inside the dim corridors, the bodies clad in military finery rest gently upon silk linings, their solemn forms now adorned in the quiet dignity of service. The coffin lids lower with soft finality, and cellophane bags holding folded flags are carefully taped to their surfaces, each crease and corner preserved like whispered promises. Papers rustle in the hushed darkness as signatures are exchanged—copies passed between hands that know the gravity of such moments all too well.

[NARRATOR] Outside, beneath the pale glow of sodium lamps, the white caskets are guided onto a military truck with reverent precision. The engine idles low, waiting patiently as the last documents are signed. Then, with measured purpose, the truck begins to move, its headlights cutting through the cool night air, carrying the fallen toward their final resting place.

[NARRATOR] Inside the hospital, two young privates in crisp uniforms walk down a corridor lined with shadows. Their footsteps echo softly against the tile floor as they make their way to a room where the original plain wooden coffins have been discarded. The door opens quietly, revealing stacks of empty vessels that once held more than earth and memory.

[NARRATOR] One private lifts the lid of a coffin with practiced efficiency, followed by the removal of a finely-crafted false bottom. Beneath lies a hidden cavity, precisely carved to hold something far removed from mourning. Tightly packed within are bricks of Double UO Globe heroin, their presence a stark contrast to the ceremony outside. The choir begins to sing then, voices rising in haunting harmony, carrying through the halls like a warning wrapped in beauty.

[NARRATOR] Morning breaks gently over the army base, painting the sky in soft hues of dawn. A laundry truck idles patiently near the loading dock, steam rising slightly from its hood in the crisp air. Stevie, the Lucas nephew whose dreams once reached toward baseball diamonds and stadium lights, hops down from the driver's seat with ease. His frame carries the strength of someone who could have worn pinstripes, but instead finds himself here, helping two privates toss several laundry bags into the back of the truck.

[NARRATOR] The engine purrs to life, and the truck rolls forward, winding its way past guard towers and through the waking base. Beyond the perimeter, a stand of trees lines the road like sentinels, their branches swaying softly in the morning breeze. Parked beside his detectives' cars, Richie watches the truck pass, recognition flashing in his eyes as he takes in the familiar sight of the young driver wearing that same Yankee baseball cap from his personnel file.

[NARRATOR] The detectives exchange glances before climbing into their vehicles. Two Lucas cars fold in behind the laundry truck, maintaining careful distance as they begin to follow. The pursuit has begun, quiet and deliberate, each man aware that today may bring answers long sought.

[NARRATOR] The gospel choir continues to sing as the morning sun climbs higher, casting long shadows across the pavement. In the distance, the baptist church stands proud against the sky, its steps filled with congregants gathering for services. Hats in every shade of spring bloom nod gracefully as the choir's voices blend with the rustling of fabric and whispered prayers.

[NARRATOR] Inside the sanctuary, the sea of ladies' hats sways gently with the rhythm of the music. No matter what storms may rage in his complicated world, Frank finds peace here, sitting beside his mother and Ana in their usual pew. The gospel music flows like warm honey, wrapping around them in comfort and tradition.

[NARRATOR] As the final notes fade, Frank rises from his seat, his movements measured and deliberate. He steps out into the morning light, where his mother and Ana await. With gentle care, he kisses each of their cheeks, offering silent blessings before settling them into the care of a waiting driver. The car pulls away slowly, leaving Frank standing alone on the church steps, the weight of his choices resting heavily on his shoulders as he climbs into his own vehicle.

[NARRATOR] The morning light settles gently over the city, soft and diffused, as if the sun itself moves with quiet purpose. Doc drives alone now, the rhythm of the road steady beneath the wheels, each turn of the steering wheel a slow, deliberate motion. His mother stands at the window, her silhouette framed by the pale glow of dawn, her thoughts drifting like leaves on a slow current. She wonders, perhaps, if he means to settle something final with Trupo after all. The weight of such a question lingers in the air, unspoken but felt. As Doc's car fades into the distance, another follows—not in haste, but with the same measured intent, as though the path ahead has already been laid bare.

[NARRATOR] The highway stretches forward, smooth and endless, flanked by the quiet hum of early traffic. The laundry truck moves steadily ahead, its cargo secured, its course unwavering. Richie trails behind, a few car lengths distant, watching the way the road bends toward the great stone arches of the George Washington Bridge. The morning air carries the scent of dew-kissed grass and distant exhaust, mingling in soft layers. The truck does not falter, does not hesitate—it simply continues, as if it has all the time in the world.

[NARRATOR] Above, the view spreads wide and open. From the heights, the convergence becomes clear—the laundry truck, the gun car, the van, and the detectives' vehicles, each approaching from its own direction, like rivers drawn inexorably toward a single sea. The city awakens slowly around them, buildings casting long shadows across the pavement, their edges softened by the morning mist. The sound of tires on asphalt is the only music here, steady and unbroken.

[NARRATOR] The turn comes without warning, sharp and sudden, yet somehow inevitable. Red Top's van swings wide, its driver intent on the path ahead. Behind it, Abruzzo's car mirrors the motion, his hands steady on the wheel, his eyes fixed on the darkening shape rising from the horizon. The Stephen Crane Projects loom large in his windshield, their concrete towers standing like sentinels against the pale sky. He eases off the gas, not from fear, but from recognition—this place holds stories too heavy for casual passing. The gun car glides past, indifferent to the weight of history, and in its wake, Jones slows, then stops. Teddy's car appears from another angle, pulled over now, its engine ticking softly in the stillness. One by one, they come to rest at the threshold of the foreboding grounds, as if even the machines understand the gravity of what waits within.

[NARRATOR] The gospel music fades into silence, leaving only the whisper of wind through chain-link fences and the distant echo of footsteps. The moment hangs suspended, thick with unspoken resolve. Richie stands apart, phone pressed to his ear, his voice low but steady.

[RICHIE] Lou. We're ready to go in there knowing there's a good chance we won't all come out. That's what we're willing to do. All I'm asking you to do is get me a warrant.

[NARRATOR] The line holds, quiet except for the faint crackle of static. Somewhere in the distance, a dog barks once, then falls silent. The weight of his request settles between them like dust in sunlight.

[RICHIE] We don't have a lot of time to fuck around.

[NARRATOR] The words hang in the air, heavy with urgency. Across the street, the South Tower rises dark and unmoving, its windows reflecting nothing but the sky above. In his pocket, Richie feels the weight of the decision pressing close, like a stone worn smooth by years of carrying. The morning deepens around him, indifferent and eternal.

[NARRATOR] The phone call fades into silence, leaving only the hum of the line and the distant echo of footsteps in the hallway.

[MALE] I'll call in the warrant. And some backup. Don't go in before either gets there.

[NARRATOR] The connection breaks, and the receiver clicks back into place. Outside, the morning light filters through the windows of the apartment, soft and gray, as the girls move quietly about their task. They spread rolls of plastic sheeting across tabletops, taping the edges down with careful, methodical hands. The sound of tape tearing is sharp, then muffled, as they begin to change for work, which means slowly, deliberately, removing their clothes. The air grows cooler, and the only light comes from the street below, casting long, pale rectangles across the floor.

[NARRATOR] In the distance, the sound of engines idles. Richie sits in his car, hands resting on the steering wheel, watching the street. His men fan out, checking their watches, shifting in their seats. The neighborhood stirs around them, but their attention is fixed on the building ahead.

[MALE] How long we gonna wait for it?

[NARRATOR] Jones shifts uncomfortably, glancing at the clock on his dashboard. Richie doesn't answer right away. He just keeps staring down the street, where the buildings rise like sentinels, their windows dark except for a few scattered lights. The silence stretches, broken only by the occasional bark of a dog or the hum of a passing car.

[MALE] It'll be here.

[NARRATOR] Inside the apartment, the pharmaceutical scales sit on the counter, their brass weights gleaming in the dim light. The five women move between the tables with practiced precision, their bare feet silent on the linoleum. They cut the heroin with quinine, measuring each portion to Frank's exacting standards. The powder is fine, almost luminous in the morning glow, and the air carries a faint, metallic tang. Red Top stands by the coffee pot, stirring slowly, watching the steam rise from the cup.

[NARRATOR] A single blade slices through a sheet of blue cellophane, the sound sharp and clean. The girls bend over their work, their fingers moving with the expertise of Cuban cigar makers, wrapping pieces of the cellophane around precisely-measured quarter-ounce drifts of Black Magic. Each bundle is tied with thin string, twisted tight, and placed carefully in rows. The room fills with the quiet scratch of paper, the soft rustle of cellophane, and the occasional whisper of breath.

[NARRATOR] Frank's car winds up the winding road of the cemetery, the engine a low purr beneath the trees. The headstones stand like sleeping soldiers, their inscriptions worn smooth by time. The surveillance car follows behind, slipping through the main gate with barely a sound. It parks near the edge of the lot, and the men inside settle in, watching the road.

[NARRATOR] Back at the projects, Richie's jaw tightens. He knows the weight of waiting, the way it presses against your ribs until you can't tell if it's fear or anticipation. Spearman checks his watch again, then looks away. The street is empty except for a few early risers, their footsteps echoing off the brick walls.

[NARRATOR] Then, the sound of engines. Several black and white cars roll slowly through the neighborhood, their sirens off, their lights hidden behind tinted glass. They move like ghosts, slipping between the buildings, past the stoops where old men sit with their coffee, past the mothers pushing strollers, past the kids already racing their bikes down the sidewalk.

[NARRATOR] Toback climbs out of one of the cars, adjusting his coat against the morning chill. He walks with purpose, his footsteps measured, and hands Richie the search warrant. The paper is crisp, official, and Richie takes it without a word. The weight of it settles in his palm, and for a moment, he simply holds it there, feeling the gravity of what comes next.

[NARRATOR] The tall buildings cast long shadows across the courtyard, deepening the quiet that already hangs over everything. A woman pushes a stroller, her gaze fixed ahead. Children ride their bikes in circles, laughing, while old men rest on a graffiti-covered bench, watching the world go by.

[NARRATOR] The unmarked cars drift slowly through it all, their presence a whisper of authority in a place that teeters on the edge. You can feel it in the air, that tension, that sense that violence is never far away, that gangs move like shadows beneath the towers, always watching, always waiting.

[NARRATOR] Richie removes the cover plate of the elevator, his tool cutting through the metal with a sharp, precise motion. He cuts the wires, one by one, disengaging the mechanism. The sound is mechanical, final. Then he leads the Amigos, followed by more detectives, into the building. The door closes behind them with a soft click, and the hallway falls silent once more, save for the distant hum of the city waking up.

[NARRATOR] The stairwell breathes with the weight of forgotten things, each step worn smooth by time and trouble. Debris litters the narrow passage, broken glass and torn paper scattered as if a storm had passed through long ago. The light filters in weakly from above, casting long shadows that flicker and dance with every breath of wind.

[NARRATOR] Up above, the city hums its distant song, but here in this hollow corridor, silence presses close, heavy and thick. Frank moves slowly through the space, his footsteps muffled against the worn steps, each one echoing faintly into the void.

[NARRATOR] Outside, the cemetery stretches beneath a sky painted in soft grays and golds. Frank pauses beside a weathered headstone, his hands gentle as he replaces the dried flowers with fresh ones, their petals still dew-kissed from morning. He searches the ground for something to sit upon, spotting wooden folding chairs arranged neatly around a newer grave nearby.

[NARRATOR] In the distance, Doc waits by the car, patient and still, a silhouette against the fading light. The world around them slows, breathing in quiet rhythms as Frank takes a seat, his gaze drifting toward the horizon where sky meets earth in a hushed embrace.

[NARRATOR] Floor by floor, they ascend the tower like shadows gliding through darkness, each level revealing deeper layers of decay. The squalor grows denser, the walls closing in with peeling paint and water stains that resemble maps of distant lands. Voices murmur behind closed doors, carrying tales of struggle and survival, while the weight of hopelessness clings to every surface.

[NARRATOR] At the seventeenth floor, a strange sound catches their attention—a child’s laughter perhaps, or the rhythmic squeal of wheels on concrete. They pause, listening as the noise grows clearer, then softer, until a small figure appears around the corner, pedaling furiously on a bright red Big Wheel. His eyes meet theirs briefly before he turns away, disappearing down the hall, leaving only the fading echo of his joy behind.

[NARRATOR] The hallway stretches endlessly before them now, lined with apartment doors—some hanging open, others missing entirely. [NARRATOR] Spearman steps forward without hesitation, his jaw set in determination. With a slight nod from his partner, he moves toward the armed men, his boots clicking against the linoleum floor in steady cadence.

[MALE] Spearman approaches the pair, keeping his voice steady despite the tension coiling tightly within his chest.

[MALE] I got to talk to Teddy.

[MALE] The taller of the two gunmen sneers, his grip tightening around the weapon’s stock.

[MALE] Get the fuck out of here.

[NARRATOR] But Spearman does not budge, his eyes locked onto the man’s face, searching for any sign of weakness beneath the bravado.

[MALE] What the fuck is that? I got business with Teddy and it's none of your fuckin business except to knock on the fuckin door and get him.

[NARRATOR] With a deafening blast, the shotgun discharges, spraying plaster and pellets across the hallway like confetti at a macabre celebration. Jones and Abruzzo spring into action instantly, tackling the second gunman while Richie raises his sledgehammer high above his head, ready to breach the door that holds secrets dangerous enough to kill.

[NARRATOR] The wooden barrier splinters under the force of impact, flying aside in splinters that scatter like autumn leaves caught in an unexpected breeze. Inside, chaos reigns supreme—Teddy lunges for the bedroom, panic flashing across his features like lightning illuminating storm clouds.

[NARRATOR] The detectives storm into the cramped apartment, shouting commands urging the women present to take cover. Pain flashes through his body like fire, yet he stumbles forward, returning fire blindly toward whatever shadow dares threaten him further.

[NARRATOR] Through it all, time seems suspended—each heartbeat echoing louder than gunfire, each breath drawing deeper meaning from the madness surrounding them.
